PINGBOARD — COMPLETE PROJECT CONTEXT
Multi-tenant uptime monitoring with public status pages
Bundled 2026-10-05

This single file contains the ENTIRE project: the handover notes, the original
implementation plan, and the full source of every file, in reading order.

Not included (regenerable or secret):
  - node_modules/, dist/, __pycache__/ and other build output
  - package-lock.json (74k lines of dependency pins; run `npm install`)
  - .env (contains secrets; .env.example is included and documents every var)
  - frontend/src/api/schema.d.ts (2,651 lines GENERATED from the API's OpenAPI
    document; regenerate with `npm run generate:api` while the API is running)

To rebuild the project from this file you would need to recreate each file at
the path given in its header. Easier: unzip the real project folder, which this
file sits inside.

################################################################################
# PART 1 — HANDOVER NOTES
################################################################################

================================================================================
PINGBOARD — PROJECT HANDOVER
Multi-tenant uptime monitoring with public status pages
Built 2026-09-29 to 2026-10-05 · Phases 0-7 complete, Phase 8 not started
================================================================================

If you only read one section, read "HOW TO RUN IT" and "GOTCHAS". Everything
else is context.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. WHAT IT IS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

An organisation signs up, registers monitors (a URL + check interval + a set of
assertions), and a background worker checks them on schedule. Failures open
incidents automatically and notify the team over Slack / Discord / email. Each
org can publish a public status page that anyone can view without logging in.

Think a small, self-hostable UptimeRobot / Better Uptime.

Stack:
  Backend    FastAPI (fully async), SQLAlchemy 2.1, Alembic, Pydantic v2
  Worker     ARQ (Redis-backed, async-native)
  Database   PostgreSQL 16
  Cache/Queue Redis 7
  Frontend   React 19 + TypeScript + Vite 7 + Tailwind v4 + TanStack Query
  Charts     Recharts
  Infra      Docker Compose (5 services)

Size: ~5,700 lines backend app, ~4,300 lines frontend, ~1,000 lines tests.
41 API operations across 32 paths. 6 migrations. 215 tests.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
2. HOW TO RUN IT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

You need Docker with the Compose plugin. Nothing else — no local Python or Node.

    cp .env.example .env
    # Edit .env and set a real JWT_SECRET:
    #   openssl rand -hex 32
    # Also set DOCKER_UID / DOCKER_GID to your own (run: id -u  and  id -g)

    docker compose up --build

Then:
    Frontend        http://localhost:5173
    API             http://localhost:8000
    API docs        http://localhost:8000/docs      (Swagger, auto-generated)
    Liveness        http://localhost:8000/health
    Readiness       http://localhost:8000/health/ready   (pings DB + Redis)

Postgres is published on host port 5433 and Redis on 6380 — deliberately NOT
the standard 5432/6379, so the stack does not collide with a Postgres or Redis
you already have installed. Containers always reach each other on db:5432 and
redis:6379 regardless. Override POSTGRES_PORT / REDIS_PORT in .env if those are
taken too.

Migrations run automatically when the api container starts. To drive Alembic by
hand:

    docker compose exec api alembic upgrade head
    docker compose exec api alembic downgrade -1
    docker compose exec api alembic revision --autogenerate -m "message"
    docker compose exec api alembic check        # detects model/schema drift

Running the checks:

    docker compose exec api ruff check .
    docker compose exec api ruff format --check .
    docker compose exec api mypy app             # strict mode
    docker compose exec api pytest

    docker compose exec web npm run lint
    docker compose exec web npm run typecheck
    docker compose exec web npm run build


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
3. THE FIVE SERVICES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  db        postgres:16-alpine
  redis     redis:7-alpine          (job queue + cache + rate-limit counters)
  api       FastAPI via uvicorn     (serves HTTP, never makes outbound checks)
  worker    same image as api,      (makes outbound checks, never serves HTTP)
            different command: arq app.workers.main.WorkerSettings
  web       Vite dev server         (production target builds static + nginx)

api and worker share one image and one codebase but are separate processes.
That split is deliberate: a slow outbound HTTP check must never be able to
block an API request, and vice versa.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
4. HOW THE INTERESTING PARTS WORK
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Authentication — split-token scheme

                    Access token          Refresh token
  Form              Signed JWT (HS256)    Opaque 64-byte random string
  Lifetime          15 minutes            30 days
  Lives in          Client MEMORY only    httpOnly SameSite=Lax cookie, /auth
  Server-side       Nothing stored        SHA-256 digest row, revocable

The access token is deliberately kept OUT of localStorage: anything JavaScript
can read, injected script can read too. Because it lives only in memory, a page
reload starts with no token — the SPA silently calls POST /auth/refresh, which
the browser answers from a cookie the page itself cannot read.

Refresh tokens are SINGLE-USE. Every refresh revokes the presented token and
issues a new one. Presenting an already-rotated token is treated as theft: all
of that user's sessions are revoked and a warning is logged.

Passwords are bcrypt-hashed after a SHA-256 pre-hash. The pre-hash sidesteps
bcrypt's 72-byte truncation so a long passphrase keeps its full entropy.

GitHub OAuth is wired (authorization-code flow, state stored in Redis and burned
on use). The callback sets the refresh cookie and redirects to the SPA, so no
token ever appears in a URL. Leave GITHUB_CLIENT_ID/SECRET unset and those two
endpoints return 503; nothing else is affected.

### Roles

Everything hangs off an organisation. Access is decided by the caller's
membership row, NEVER by an org_id supplied in the request (that is only ever a
lookup key).

  viewer   read the org and its members
  admin    ...plus create/edit monitors, invite members, manage channels
  owner    ...plus everything, including granting owner and revoking API keys

Non-members get 404, not 403 — telling an outsider that an organisation exists
is itself a disclosure.

### The scheduler (worker)

Every 10 seconds the worker claims monitors whose next_run_at has passed and
enqueues one check job each. Two details make this safe with more than one
worker running:

  - The claim uses SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, so each due monitor goes
    to exactly one worker and contention never blocks.
  - next_run_at advances AS PART OF THE CLAIM, before the check runs. A worker
    that dies mid-check costs one missed data point rather than re-claiming the
    same monitor in a tight loop against someone else's server.

After an outage the scheduler catches up in a single hop rather than firing
every missed slot. A 30-second monitor unchecked for an hour runs once, not 120
times.

### Flap protection

A monitor flips to "down" only after 3 consecutive failures, so one blip does
not raise an alarm. Recovery is immediate — the first success restores "up" and
resets the counter. Both transitions are edge-triggered, which is what lets
incidents open and resolve exactly once.

### SSRF guard — the security-critical bit

A monitor is, by design, a server-side fetch of a URL the user chose. Without a
guard that is a textbook SSRF primitive: the worker sits inside your private
network and would happily fetch the cloud metadata service or an internal admin
panel on the attacker's behalf.

The guard is RESOLVE-THEN-INSPECT, not pattern matching. A hostname like
"metadata.evil.example" can resolve to 169.254.169.254, so validating the string
alone proves nothing. Every address the hostname resolves to must be public.

Rejected: loopback (all of 127/8), RFC1918 private ranges, link-local and cloud
metadata (169.254/16), carrier-grade NAT, TEST-NET ranges, multicast, reserved,
unspecified, IPv4-mapped IPv6 (::ffff:127.0.0.1), non-http(s) schemes, and
credentials embedded in the URL.

It runs THREE times: on create, on update, and again inside the worker just
before each request. The third is the important one — DNS can change between
saving a monitor and checking it, and re-resolving at execution time is what
closes the rebinding hole.

58 unit tests cover it (tests/test_ssrf.py).

### Incidents and alerting

When a monitor crosses the failure threshold the worker opens an Incident; when
it recovers, the same edge closes it and appends an automatic "resolved" entry.

A partial unique index (monitor_id WHERE resolved_at IS NULL) means the DATABASE
guarantees at most one open incident per monitor — two workers racing cannot
produce duplicates.

Notifications are QUEUED, never inline. The checker enqueues a job and moves on;
a webhook that hangs for 30 seconds cannot slow down monitoring. Delivery fans
out to every active channel, and one broken channel does not stop the others.

The one exception is POST /channels/{id}/test, which delivers synchronously
because someone configuring a webhook needs immediate feedback.

Webhook URLs are bearer credentials — anyone holding one can post into that
workspace. They are stored as given but MASKED on every read, so GET /channels
returns "https://hooks.slack.com/..." and never the secret path.

### Public status pages

GET /public/status/{slug} takes no auth dependency and returns a schema written
for that endpoint alone — never the internal MonitorRead. A visitor sees a
display name, a traffic light and a 90-day bar. The target URL, every UUID, the
assertions and the check interval all stay private. A new monitor field is
private by default rather than exposed by accident.

On the frontend the route sits OUTSIDE AuthLayout, so an anonymous visitor
triggers no session bootstrap and no call to /auth/refresh at all.

A missing slug and an unpublished page return the same 404, so the endpoint
cannot be used to discover private slugs. Monitors added to a page are filtered
through the owning org, so a guessed id from another tenant is silently dropped.

Uptime is aggregated in SQL (generate_series left-joined to a grouped count),
not by pulling rows into Python: a 90-day window on a 30-second monitor is
~250,000 rows to produce 90 numbers. Days with no checks render as explicit
gaps rather than disappearing — a monitoring outage must never masquerade as
uptime.

### Rollups and retention

A worker job aggregates each closed hour of check_results into one
rollups_hourly row per monitor, upserted on (monitor_id, hour) so a re-run after
a crash recomputes rather than double-counting.

GET /monitors/{id}/uptime reads those rollups plus the still-open hour. Measured
on 43,000 raw rows over 30 days, this cut shared buffer reads from 841 to 15.

A daily job prunes raw checks older than RAW_RETENTION_DAYS (default 30), in
batches, and only rows already covered by a rollup — so pruning can never
destroy history that was not summarised first.

### Caching and rate limiting

Public status pages (30s TTL) and uptime figures (60s TTL) are cached in Redis.
TTL-only, no invalidation: these are continuously changing aggregates where a
short staleness window is invisible. Responses carry X-Cache: HIT|MISS.

A Redis outage degrades to "slower", never "broken" — every path falls back to
computing the value.

Three separate rate-limit budgets, all Redis fixed-window by client IP:
  auth endpoints     10/min   (tightest — these are worth brute-forcing)
  public status      60/min
  authenticated writes 120/min

A Redis failure fails OPEN on purpose: losing a rate limiter must not lock users
out of signing in.

### API keys

pb_-prefixed (greppable in logs, detectable by secret scanners), shown exactly
once at creation, stored only as a SHA-256 digest. A key authenticates as an
admin of ONE organisation and is refused against any other, so POST /monitors is
scriptable from CI with no browser session:

    curl -X POST "http://localhost:8000/monitors?org_id=<ORG>" \
      -H "X-API-Key: pb_..." \
      -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
      -d '{"name":"From CI","url":"https://example.com"}'

Revoking is a soft delete, so the audit trail outlives the credential.

### Frontend

One token system. Every colour is a ROLE — surface / ink / line, plus the
reserved status colours up / down / degraded / unknown — defined once in
index.css. Dark mode is a second set of values for those same roles under
[data-theme="dark"], not a second set of components. The latency chart follows
it through var() in SVG fills.

Status is ALWAYS word + shape, never colour alone, so it survives
colour-blindness and forced-colours mode.

src/api/schema.d.ts is GENERATED from the live OpenAPI document:

    docker compose exec web npx openapi-typescript http://api:8000/openapi.json \
      -o src/api/schema.d.ts

types.ts aliases those generated schemas rather than restating them, so a
backend field that changes shape fails tsc instead of breaking at runtime.
Wiring this up immediately caught two fields the hand-written types had wrongly
assumed were always present.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
5. DATABASE SCHEMA (15 tables)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  users                  id, email UNIQUE, password_hash NULL, github_id NULL,
                         name, created_at
  refresh_tokens         id, user_id FK, token_hash UNIQUE, expires_at,
                         revoked_at NULL, created_at
  organizations          id, name, slug UNIQUE, created_at
  memberships            id, org_id FK, user_id FK, role, UNIQUE(org_id,user_id)
  org_invites            id, org_id FK, email, role, token_hash UNIQUE,
                         expires_at, accepted_at NULL, invited_by FK

  monitors               id, org_id FK, name, url, method, interval_s,
                         timeout_ms, assertions JSONB, is_active, status,
                         consecutive_failures, last_checked_at, next_run_at
  check_results          id, monitor_id FK, checked_at, ok, status_code NULL,
                         latency_ms NULL, error NULL
  rollups_hourly         monitor_id FK, hour, checks, failures, p95_ms
                         PRIMARY KEY(monitor_id, hour)

  incidents              id, monitor_id FK, started_at, resolved_at NULL,
                         severity, cause, created_at
  incident_updates       id, incident_id FK, message, created_by FK NULL,
                         is_auto, created_at
  notification_channels  id, org_id FK, name, type, config JSONB, is_active

  status_pages           id, org_id FK, slug UNIQUE, title, description,
                         is_published, created_at
  status_page_monitors   status_page_id FK, monitor_id FK (composite PK)

  api_keys               id, org_id FK, name, key_hash UNIQUE, last_used_at,
                         revoked_at NULL, created_at

  alembic_version        (Alembic bookkeeping)

Indexes worth knowing about:
  ix_monitors_due                     PARTIAL: (next_run_at) WHERE is_active
                                      — the scheduler's hot query; paused
                                      monitors cost nothing to skip
  ix_check_results_monitor_checked    (monitor_id, checked_at DESC) — history is
                                      always read newest-first, so no sort
  uq_incidents_one_open_per_monitor   PARTIAL UNIQUE: (monitor_id)
                                      WHERE resolved_at IS NULL

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
7. WHAT WAS BUILT, PHASE BY PHASE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Built against PingBoard_Implementation_Plan.md (the file one directory up), which
splits the work into 9 phases. Each phase has an acceptance test; each was
verified before moving on, by driving the real app in a headless Chrome and with
real HTTP calls.

Phase 0  Scaffolding        docker compose up boots everything; browser renders
                            {"status":"ok"} from the API
Phase 1  Auth & orgs        Register -> invite -> accept; tampered JWT rejected
                            with 401; Org A's token cannot read Org B
Phase 2  Monitors & checker Create monitor + Run now; localhost,
                            169.254.169.254 and ftp:// all rejected
Phase 3  Scheduler & worker Checks accumulate untouched; worker stopped -> none
                            appear; status flips down only at 3 failures
Phase 4  Incidents & alerts Incident auto-opened at the threshold, webhook
                            fired, auto-resolved on recovery
Phase 5  Public status page Incognito context loads it with ZERO /auth/ calls
                            and no UUIDs anywhere in the DOM
Phase 6  Scale & safety     Rollups cut 841 buffer reads to 15; API key creates
                            a monitor via curl, then revoked -> 401
Phase 7  Polish & UX        Dashboard, dark mode, toasts, 390px with no
                            horizontal overflow, generated typed client

Phase 8 (testing, CI/CD, deployment) was deliberately NOT started.
Phase 9 (stretch features) likewise.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
8. BUGS FOUND AND FIXED DURING THE BUILD
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

The two worth knowing about, both real and both found by testing rather than by
reading:

(a) CONCURRENT REFRESH REVOKED EVERY SESSION.
    AuthProvider's mount effect called /auth/refresh directly, bypassing the
    single-flight dedupe that only covered 401-triggered refreshes. React
    StrictMode double-invokes that effect, so two concurrent refreshes fired;
    the second looked like token reuse and triggered mass revocation. This would
    also have hit two browser tabs opening at the same time in production.
    Fixed by routing EVERY refresh through one shared promise. Verified with
    three simultaneous tab loads and zero reuse events.

(b) PUBLIC STATUS PAGE HUNG ON "Loading...".
    A failed auth bootstrap called queryClient.clear(), which aborted the page's
    own in-flight request. Fixed by only clearing when a session actually ended,
    and by moving the public route outside AuthProvider entirely.

Also fixed along the way:
  - Postgres enum types orphaned on downgrade (affected 3 migrations). An
    implicit CREATE TYPE from a column definition is never paired with a
    DROP TYPE, so downgrade-then-upgrade failed. All enums are now created and
    dropped explicitly.
  - Annotated[..., dependency] without Depends() silently became a query
    parameter, making POST /incidents/{id}/updates return 422 always.
  - Slug truncation left a trailing hyphen (caught by a test written for it).
  - date_trunc overload ambiguity with asyncpg — bind parameters arrive untyped,
    so the cast has to be explicit.
  - User.memberships was eager-loading on every authenticated request for a
    relationship nothing read.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
9. GOTCHAS — READ THIS BEFORE YOU ARE CONFUSED
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

* SELinux (Fedora/RHEL). Bind mounts need the :z label or the container gets
  PermissionError reading its own source. docker-compose.yml already sets it. If
  you add a mount, label it too.

* File ownership. Both images take UID/GID build args. Set DOCKER_UID and
  DOCKER_GID in .env to your own values (id -u, id -g) or files the containers
  write will be owned by the wrong user.

* The auth rate limiter WILL bite you while testing. 10 requests/minute per IP
  on /auth/login and /auth/register. If a test script suddenly starts failing
  after a few runs, that is almost certainly why. Wait 60 seconds.

* p95 from rollups is APPROXIMATE. Counts recombine exactly (verified:
  43,153 = 43,153). Percentiles do not — you cannot recover a true window p95
  from per-hour p95s. The reported figure is the worst hour's p95, a deliberate
  over-estimate (measured 212 true vs 216 reported) rather than a number that
  looks exact and is quietly wrong. Query raw rows for a true p95 inside the
  retention window.

* Webhook URLs accept plain http:// when ENV != prod, so a local receiver works
  in development. Production requires https://.

* example.com occasionally resolves to a TEST-NET address (192.0.2.1) depending
  on the resolver. The SSRF guard correctly blocks that. If monitor creation
  against example.com suddenly 422s, check DNS before suspecting a bug.

* The refresh cookie is scoped to Path=/auth and SameSite=Lax. If you deploy the
  frontend and API to genuinely different sites (e.g. *.vercel.app calling
  *.fly.dev) you must set COOKIE_SAMESITE=none and COOKIE_SECURE=true. Different
  ports on one host are still same-site, so lax is correct locally.

* Monitors are org-scoped via an org_id QUERY parameter, not a path segment.
  That parameter is a lookup key only — authorisation always comes from the
  caller's membership row.


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
10. WHAT IS NOT DONE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Phase 8 is entirely untouched. Specifically missing:

  - DB integration tests. The 215 existing tests are unit-level: the SSRF guard,
    the checker's assertion logic, the scheduler's next_run_at arithmetic, role
    comparison, slugify, the notifier, rollup maths, API key hashing. There are
    NO tests that exercise auth or monitor CRUD against a real database.
  - Frontend tests. No Vitest, no React Testing Library, no Playwright. All
    frontend verification so far was done by driving headless Chrome manually
    during the build; those scripts were scratch files and are not in the repo.
  - CI. No GitHub Actions workflow. The lint/type/test commands all exist and
    pass, but nothing runs them automatically.
  - Deployment. No docker-compose.prod.yml, no platform config, no secret
    manager wiring. The frontend Dockerfile HAS a production target (static
    build + nginx with SPA fallback and immutable asset caching) but it has
    never been exercised.
  - No live deployment, so no demo URL and no screenshots in the README.

Phase 9 stretch ideas (SSL expiry checks, heartbeat monitors, multi-region
checkers, AI postmortem drafts, Prometheus /metrics, maintenance windows) are
listed in the plan and none are started.

Other known gaps:
  - Email is console-logged unless SMTP_HOST is set. No Mailhog in compose.
  - No way to change a member's role or remove a member after they join.
  - No password reset flow.
  - Incident severity is always "major" — the column and enum exist, nothing
    sets anything else.

    notify.py               notification fan-out job
    maintenance.py          hourly rollup + daily prune jobs
    queue.py                enqueueing from the API process

    StatusPill, LatencyChart, UptimeBar, format.ts

12. VERIFICATION STATUS AS OF HANDOVER
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Verified from a completely wiped cold start (docker compose down -v, then
up --build):

  [x] 15 tables built from 6 migrations on an empty database
  [x] All 5 services healthy
  [x] Worker scheduling and executing checks
  [x] 215 tests passing
  [x] ruff check / ruff format --check clean
  [x] mypy --strict clean (66 source files)
  [x] eslint clean
  [x] tsc clean
  [x] vite build succeeds
  [x] pip-audit: no known vulnerabilities
  [x] npm audit: 0 vulnerabilities
  [x] alembic check: no model/schema drift
  [x] Every migration round-trips (upgrade -> downgrade -> upgrade)

Live smoke test on the fresh stack: register -> org -> SSRF guard rejects three
vectors -> monitor goes "up" within 8s via the worker -> public status page
readable with no auth and no leaked URL -> API key creates a monitor -> rate
limiter returns 429 after 59 requests.

================================================================================
END
================================================================================


################################################################################
# PART 2 — THE ORIGINAL IMPLEMENTATION PLAN
################################################################################

This is the spec the project was built against, unmodified. Phases 0-7 are
complete; Phase 8 (testing/CI/CD/deployment) and Phase 9 (stretch) are not
started.

# PingBoard — Implementation Plan

A multi-tenant uptime monitoring platform with public status pages.
**Stack:** React + TypeScript + Tailwind · FastAPI (async) · PostgreSQL · Redis · Docker · JWT/OAuth

This plan is broken into **9 phases**, each independently demo-able. Each phase has:
- **Goal** — what "done" looks like
- **Tasks** — checkboxes to track as you build
- **DB changes** — migrations to write
- **API endpoints** — the contract for that phase
- **LLM prompting tips** — how to prompt effectively for that slice of work
- **Acceptance test** — how you personally verify the phase before moving on

Work phase by phase, in order. Don't start Phase N+1 until Phase N's acceptance test passes. When vibe-coding with an LLM, paste the relevant phase section (goal + tasks + DB + endpoints) as context so the model doesn't over- or under-build.

---

## Table of Contents

- [Phase 0 — Project Scaffolding & Environment](#phase-0--project-scaffolding--environment)
- [Phase 1 — Auth & Organizations](#phase-1--auth--organizations)
- [Phase 2 — Monitors CRUD & Checker Core](#phase-2--monitors-crud--checker-core)
- [Phase 3 — Scheduler & Background Worker](#phase-3--scheduler--background-worker)
- [Phase 4 — Incidents & Alerting](#phase-4--incidents--alerting)
- [Phase 5 — Public Status Pages](#phase-5--public-status-pages)
- [Phase 6 — Rollups, Caching, Rate Limiting, API Keys](#phase-6--rollups-caching-rate-limiting-api-keys)
- [Phase 7 — Frontend Polish & UX](#phase-7--frontend-polish--ux)
- [Phase 8 — Testing, CI/CD, Deployment](#phase-8--testing-cicd-deployment)
- [Phase 9 — Stretch / Advanced Features](#phase-9--stretch--advanced-features)

- [Appendix B — Full API Reference](#appendix-b--full-api-reference)

## Phase 0 — Project Scaffolding & Environment

**Goal:** A running skeleton — `docker compose up` boots API, DB, Redis, and a frontend shell that talks to a `/health` endpoint.

### Tasks
- [ ] Create monorepo structure:
  ```
  pingboard/
  ├─ backend/
  │  ├─ app/
  │  │  ├─ main.py
  │  │  ├─ core/
  │  │  │  ├─ config.py        # pydantic Settings, reads env vars
  │  │  │  ├─ db.py             # async engine + session factory
  │  │  │  ├─ security.py       # JWT + password hashing helpers
  │  │  │  └─ logging.py
  │  │  ├─ auth/
  │  │  ├─ orgs/
  │  │  ├─ monitors/
  │  │  ├─ incidents/
  │  │  ├─ channels/
  │  │  ├─ status_pages/
  │  │  └─ workers/
  │  ├─ alembic/
  │  ├─ tests/
  │  ├─ pyproject.toml
  │  └─ Dockerfile
  ├─ frontend/
  │  ├─ src/
  │  │  ├─ api/
  │  │  ├─ features/
  │  │  ├─ components/
  │  │  ├─ hooks/
  │  │  ├─ pages/
  │  │  └─ routes.tsx
  │  ├─ package.json
  │  └─ Dockerfile
  ├─ docker-compose.yml
  ├─ .env.example
  └─ README.md
  ```
- [ ] Backend: FastAPI app with `/health` returning `{"status": "ok"}`.
- [ ] Backend: `pyproject.toml` with `fastapi`, `uvicorn[standard]`, `sqlalchemy[asyncio]`, `asyncpg`, `alembic`, `pydantic-settings`, `python-jose`, `passlib[bcrypt]`, `httpx`, `redis`, `arq` (or `celery` + `redis`), `ruff`, `mypy`, `pytest`, `pytest-asyncio`.
- [ ] Backend: `core/config.py` — `Settings` class loading `DATABASE_URL`, `REDIS_URL`, `JWT_SECRET`, `JWT_ALGORITHM`, `ACCESS_TOKEN_EXPIRE_MINUTES`, `REFRESH_TOKEN_EXPIRE_DAYS`, `GITHUB_CLIENT_ID/SECRET`, `SMTP_*`, `ENV`.
- [ ] Backend: `core/db.py` — async SQLAlchemy engine, `AsyncSession` dependency (`get_db`).
- [ ] Alembic initialized and configured to read `DATABASE_URL` from settings, `env.py` set up for async migrations.
- [ ] Frontend: Vite + React + TypeScript scaffold, Tailwind configured, TanStack Query provider, React Router, a base `apiClient` (axios or fetch wrapper) pointing at `VITE_API_URL`.
- [ ] Frontend: one page that calls `/health` and displays the result, to prove the wiring works end-to-end.
- [ ] `docker-compose.yml` with services: `db` (postgres:16), `redis`, `api` (backend, hot-reload volume mount), `worker` (same image, different command, added in Phase 3), `web` (frontend dev server or built static + nginx).
- [ ] `.env.example` documenting every variable used.
- [ ] `README.md` — one-paragraph project description, architecture diagram (ASCII is fine), local setup instructions (`docker compose up --build`).

### DB changes
- None yet — just confirm Alembic can connect and run `alembic upgrade head` against an empty revision list without error.

### API endpoints
- `GET /health` → `{"status": "ok"}`

### LLM prompting tips
- Ask for the scaffold in one shot: *"Generate a FastAPI project skeleton with async SQLAlchemy, Alembic, pydantic-settings, and a /health endpoint, following this exact folder structure: [paste structure]."*
- Ask separately for the `docker-compose.yml` and Dockerfiles — mixing app code and infra config in one prompt tends to produce sloppier Dockerfiles.
- Explicitly say "async SQLAlchemy 2.0 style with `AsyncSession`, not the legacy `Session`" — models drift to sync patterns otherwise.

### Acceptance test
`docker compose up --build` from a clean clone → visiting the frontend shows a page confirming `{"status": "ok"}` from the API. `alembic upgrade head` runs cleanly.

---

## Phase 1 — Auth & Organizations

**Goal:** A user can register, log in, receive a JWT, create an organization, and invite a teammate. Every subsequent resource will hang off `org_id`.

### Tasks
- [ ] `core/security.py`: password hashing (`passlib` bcrypt), `create_access_token`, `create_refresh_token`, `decode_token`.
- [ ] Models: `User`, `Organization`, `Membership` (role: `owner` / `admin` / `viewer`).
- [ ] Alembic migration for the three tables above.
- [ ] `POST /auth/register` — email + password, hashes password, creates user.
- [ ] `POST /auth/login` — verifies password, issues access token (short-lived, ~15 min) + refresh token (httpOnly secure cookie, rotating, stored hashed in DB in a `refresh_tokens` table with `revoked_at`).
- [ ] `POST /auth/refresh` — reads cookie, validates against stored hash, rotates it, issues new access token.
- [ ] `POST /auth/logout` — revokes the refresh token.
- [ ] GitHub OAuth: `GET /auth/github/login` (redirect), `GET /auth/github/callback` (exchange code, upsert user by GitHub email/id, issue tokens). Use authorization code flow; PKCE if you want extra rigor.
- [ ] FastAPI dependency `get_current_user` (decodes access token, loads user) and `require_role(min_role)` dependency factory for RBAC.
- [ ] `POST /orgs` — creates org, creates membership with role `owner` for creator.
- [ ] `GET /orgs` — list orgs the current user belongs to.
- [ ] `GET /orgs/{id}/members` — list members (owner/admin only).
- [ ] `POST /orgs/{id}/invite` — creates an invite token (email + org + role, expiring), sends email (stub with console-log in dev, real SMTP later).
- [ ] `POST /orgs/invites/{token}/accept` — creates membership for the invited (now-registered) user.
- [ ] Frontend: `/register`, `/login`, `/orgs/new`, `/orgs/:id/members` pages; auth context/hook (`useAuth`) that stores the access token in memory (not localStorage) and silently refreshes on 401.
- [ ] Frontend: protected route wrapper that redirects to `/login` if unauthenticated.

### DB changes
```sql
users(id, email UNIQUE, password_hash NULL, github_id NULL, name, created_at)
refresh_tokens(id, user_id FK, token_hash, expires_at, revoked_at NULL, created_at)
organizations(id, name, slug UNIQUE, created_at)
memberships(id, org_id FK, user_id FK, role ENUM('owner','admin','viewer'), UNIQUE(org_id, user_id))
org_invites(id, org_id FK, email, role, token UNIQUE, expires_at, accepted_at NULL)
```

### API endpoints
```
POST   /auth/register
POST   /auth/login
POST   /auth/refresh
POST   /auth/logout
GET    /auth/github/login
GET    /auth/github/callback
POST   /orgs
GET    /orgs
GET    /orgs/{org_id}/members
POST   /orgs/{org_id}/invite
POST   /orgs/invites/{token}/accept
```

### LLM prompting tips
- Prompt auth in two passes: first "write the password hashing + JWT create/decode utilities with tests," then "write the register/login/refresh endpoints using those utilities." Doing both at once tends to produce inconsistent token payload shapes.
- For RBAC, give the model the exact dependency signature you want, e.g. *"a dependency `require_role('admin')` that raises 403 if the current user's membership role for `org_id` (from the path) is below the required role"* — otherwise it will invent a different pattern per router.
- Explicitly ask for refresh tokens to be **stored hashed** and **rotated on use** — models often skip rotation unless told.

### Acceptance test
Register → login → create org → invite a second (test) user by email → second user registers and accepts the invite → both users see the org in `GET /orgs`. A tampered/expired JWT is rejected with 401. An access token from Org A cannot read Org B's members.

---

## Phase 2 — Monitors CRUD & Checker Core

**Goal:** Within an org, create a monitor (URL + interval + assertions) and manually trigger one check that stores a result.

### Tasks
- [ ] Model: `Monitor` (org_id, name, url, method, interval_s, timeout_ms, assertions JSONB, is_active, next_run_at).
- [ ] Model: `CheckResult` (monitor_id, checked_at, ok, status_code, latency_ms, error).
- [ ] Migration for both, with an index on `(monitor_id, checked_at)`.
- [ ] `POST /monitors` — validate URL, **block private/internal IP ranges and non-http(s) schemes** (SSRF guard — resolve the hostname and reject loopback/link-local/private CIDRs before ever making the request).
- [ ] `GET /monitors` (scoped to org), `GET /monitors/{id}`, `PATCH /monitors/{id}`, `DELETE /monitors/{id}`.
- [ ] `monitors/checker.py` — a pure async function `run_check(monitor) -> CheckResult` using `httpx.AsyncClient`, applying timeout, following the assertion rules (status code match, latency threshold, body keyword present/absent).
- [ ] `POST /monitors/{id}/run-now` — calls the checker synchronously (for this phase only; Phase 3 makes it async/scheduled) and persists the result.
- [ ] `GET /monitors/{id}/checks?from&to` — paginated check history.
- [ ] `POST /monitors/{id}/pause` / `POST /monitors/{id}/resume`.
- [ ] Frontend: monitor list page, "add monitor" form (URL, method, interval, simple assertion builder), monitor detail page with a latency chart (Recharts) and a raw check-history table, a "Run now" button.

### DB changes
```sql
monitors(id, org_id FK, name, url, method DEFAULT 'GET', interval_s DEFAULT 60,
          timeout_ms DEFAULT 5000, assertions JSONB DEFAULT '{}', is_active BOOL DEFAULT true,
          next_run_at TIMESTAMPTZ, created_at)
check_results(id, monitor_id FK, checked_at TIMESTAMPTZ, ok BOOL,
               status_code INT NULL, latency_ms INT NULL, error TEXT NULL)
-- index: (monitor_id, checked_at DESC)
```

### API endpoints
```
POST   /monitors
GET    /monitors
GET    /monitors/{id}
PATCH  /monitors/{id}
DELETE /monitors/{id}
POST   /monitors/{id}/run-now
GET    /monitors/{id}/checks?from=&to=&limit=&cursor=
POST   /monitors/{id}/pause
POST   /monitors/{id}/resume
```

### LLM prompting tips
- Ask for the SSRF guard as its own testable unit: *"a function `is_safe_url(url: str) -> bool` that resolves the hostname and rejects loopback, link-local, private (RFC1918), and multicast ranges, plus non-http(s) schemes"* — then write a few unit tests with obviously-bad inputs (`http://169.254.169.254`, `http://localhost`, `ftp://...`) before wiring it into the endpoint.
- Give the model the exact `assertions` JSON shape you want up front, e.g. `{"status_code": 200, "max_latency_ms": 2000, "body_contains": "ok"}` so the checker and the frontend form agree on the contract.
- For the checker function, ask for it with dependency-injected `httpx.AsyncClient` (passed in, not created inside) — makes it trivial to mock in tests.

### Acceptance test
Create a monitor pointing at a real public URL (e.g. your own deployed `/health`) → "Run now" → a `CheckResult` row appears and renders in the UI. Attempt to create a monitor pointing at `http://localhost` or `http://169.254.169.254` → rejected with a clear error. Pausing a monitor is reflected in the list.

---

## Phase 3 — Scheduler & Background Worker

**Goal:** Checks run automatically on their configured interval without any manual trigger, via a separate worker process.

### Tasks
- [ ] Choose a job runner: **ARQ** (lightweight, async-native, Redis-backed) is a good fit for an all-async FastAPI stack; Celery is the more "enterprise" resume keyword if you'd rather show that.
- [ ] `workers/scheduler.py` — a loop (or ARQ cron job) that, every N seconds, queries `monitors WHERE is_active AND next_run_at <= now()`, enqueues a check job per monitor, and updates `next_run_at += interval_s`.
- [ ] `workers/runner.py` — the job handler: loads the monitor, calls `run_check`, persists the `CheckResult`, updates monitor status (`up`/`down`), and — new column — tracks `consecutive_failures`.
- [ ] Add `docker-compose` service `worker` running `arq app.workers.WorkerSettings` (separate container from `api`, same image).
- [ ] Backpressure: cap concurrent in-flight checks (e.g. `max_jobs` in ARQ settings) so one slow monitor batch can't starve others.
- [ ] Add `status` (`unknown|up|down`) and `consecutive_failures` columns to `monitors`; only flip `status` to `down` after N consecutive failures (flap protection) — this sets up Phase 4's incident logic.
- [ ] Frontend: monitor list shows live status pill (up/down/unknown) and "last checked" relative time; poll or refetch periodically (TanStack Query `refetchInterval`).

### DB changes
```sql
ALTER TABLE monitors ADD COLUMN status TEXT DEFAULT 'unknown';
ALTER TABLE monitors ADD COLUMN consecutive_failures INT DEFAULT 0;
```

### API endpoints
- No new REST endpoints this phase — internal worker plumbing only. (Optionally `GET /monitors/{id}` now also returns `status`.)

### LLM prompting tips
- Prompt the scheduler and the job handler as two separate, small functions and ask for them to be independently unit-testable (pass in a fake "now" and a fake enqueue function) — schedulers are easy to get subtly wrong (off-by-one on `next_run_at`, double-enqueueing) and small pure functions are much easier to verify than one big loop.
- Ask explicitly for **idempotency**: *"if the worker crashes after enqueueing but before updating `next_run_at`, what happens on restart?"* — have the LLM reason about this before you accept the design, don't just take the first answer.
- When wiring ARQ, ask for the exact `WorkerSettings` class and the redis connection reuse pattern — this is boilerplate the model sometimes gets wrong on version mismatches, so pin the ARQ version in your prompt.

### Acceptance test
Create a monitor with a 30-second interval, don't touch the UI, and watch check results accumulate on their own over 2–3 minutes. Stop the worker container, confirm no new checks appear (proves it's the worker, not the API, driving execution). Point a monitor at a URL you can toggle (or intentionally break), confirm `status` only flips to `down` after the configured consecutive-failure threshold, not on the first blip.

---

## Phase 4 — Incidents & Alerting

**Goal:** A monitor going down automatically opens an incident; team members can post updates; resolving auto-closes it; configured channels get notified.

### Tasks
- [ ] Model: `Incident` (monitor_id, started_at, resolved_at NULL, severity).
- [ ] Model: `IncidentUpdate` (incident_id, message, created_by, created_at).
- [ ] Model: `NotificationChannel` (org_id, type: `email`/`slack`/`discord`, config JSONB, is_active).
- [ ] In the worker's runner: when a monitor's status flips `up → down`, create an `Incident`; when it flips `down → up`, set `resolved_at` on the open incident and add an auto-generated "resolved" update.
- [ ] `channels/notifier.py` — abstraction with one implementation per channel type; Slack/Discord via incoming webhook POST, email via SMTP (or a provider like Resend).
- [ ] Enqueue a notification job whenever an incident opens, gets a manual update, or resolves — don't call external services synchronously from the worker's hot path.
- [ ] `GET /incidents` (scoped to org, filterable by monitor/status), `GET /incidents/{id}`, `POST /incidents/{id}/updates`, `POST /incidents/{id}/resolve` (manual override).
- [ ] `GET|POST /channels`, `PATCH|DELETE /channels/{id}`, `POST /channels/{id}/test` (sends a test notification synchronously so the user gets immediate feedback while configuring).
- [ ] Frontend: incident list (open/resolved tabs), incident detail with a timeline of updates, "post update" form, channel settings page with a "send test" button.

### DB changes
```sql
incidents(id, monitor_id FK, started_at, resolved_at NULL, severity DEFAULT 'major', created_at)
incident_updates(id, incident_id FK, message, created_by FK users, created_at, is_auto BOOL DEFAULT false)
notification_channels(id, org_id FK, type TEXT, config JSONB, is_active BOOL DEFAULT true, created_at)
```

### API endpoints
```
GET    /incidents?status=&monitor_id=
GET    /incidents/{id}
POST   /incidents/{id}/updates
POST   /incidents/{id}/resolve
GET    /channels
POST   /channels
PATCH  /channels/{id}
DELETE /channels/{id}
POST   /channels/{id}/test
```

### LLM prompting tips
- Ask for the notifier as an interface/protocol with `async def send(channel, message) -> None`, then one small implementation per type — this keeps Slack/Discord/email logic from tangling together, and makes it trivial to add a new channel type later.
- Be explicit that notifications are **fire-and-forget via the job queue, not inline** in the check-processing path — otherwise the LLM will happily bolt an `httpx.post` to Slack directly inside the hot loop from Phase 3, which will slow down or block your checker under load.
- Ask for retry-with-backoff on the notification job (e.g. 3 attempts, exponential) since webhook endpoints do occasionally fail transiently.

### Acceptance test
Point a monitor at a URL, then make it fail (change it to a bad path) → after the consecutive-failure threshold, an incident auto-opens and a real Slack/Discord message (or email, in dev via a tool like Mailhog) arrives. Post a manual update → notification fires. Fix the URL → incident auto-resolves with an auto-generated update, and a "resolved" notification fires.

---

## Phase 5 — Public Status Pages

**Goal:** Anyone with the link (no login) can view an org's status page showing selected monitors' current status and recent incidents.

### Tasks
- [ ] Model: `StatusPage` (org_id, slug UNIQUE, title, monitor_ids ARRAY or a join table `status_page_monitors`).
- [ ] `POST /status-pages`, `GET /status-pages` (admin-scoped, to manage), `PATCH /status-pages/{id}` (add/remove monitors, rename).
- [ ] `GET /public/status/{slug}` — **no auth** — returns page title, each included monitor's current status and 90-day uptime %, and any incidents in the last N days, all pre-shaped for direct rendering (don't leak internal fields like `url` if you don't want the raw target exposed — the spec text field can hide it, or you can simply expose it, your call, but make the decision deliberately).
- [ ] Frontend: `/status/:slug` public page (separate layout, no nav/auth chrome), each monitor as a row with a colored status dot and a 90-day uptime bar (green/red day cells), incident history below.
- [ ] Admin frontend: status page builder — pick a title/slug, select which monitors to include, preview link.

### DB changes
```sql
status_pages(id, org_id FK, slug UNIQUE, title, created_at)
status_page_monitors(status_page_id FK, monitor_id FK, PRIMARY KEY(status_page_id, monitor_id))
```

### API endpoints
```
POST   /status-pages
GET    /status-pages
PATCH  /status-pages/{id}
DELETE /status-pages/{id}
GET    /public/status/{slug}          # no auth required
```

### LLM prompting tips
- Be explicit that `/public/status/{slug}` must **never** require the auth dependency, and must **not** accidentally return any field tied to the requesting org's internal user/membership data — ask the model to define a narrow response schema (Pydantic model) for this endpoint specifically rather than reusing the internal `MonitorRead` schema, so nothing leaks by accident.
- Ask for the 90-day uptime calculation as a single reusable SQL query (aggregate `check_results` grouped by day) rather than pulling all raw rows into Python and computing in a loop — good opportunity to practice writing this as a CTE.

### Acceptance test
Log out entirely (or use a private/incognito window) and load `/status/your-slug` — it renders without hitting any auth-protected code path. Toggling a monitor off the status page in the admin UI removes it from the public view. An incident shows up on the public page while open and stays visible in history after resolution.

---

## Phase 6 — Rollups, Caching, Rate Limiting, API Keys

**Goal:** The system stays fast and safe as check volume grows: hourly aggregates instead of scanning raw rows, Redis caching on hot reads, rate limits on writes, and API keys for programmatic access.

### Tasks
- [ ] Model: `RollupHourly` (monitor_id, hour, checks, failures, p95_ms).
- [ ] Worker job (hourly cron) that aggregates the prior hour's `check_results` into one `RollupHourly` row per monitor.
- [ ] Retention job: after rollups exist for a period, optionally drop raw `check_results` older than e.g. 30 days (mention this as a design decision even if you keep raw data longer in your own deployment).
- [ ] Cache `GET /public/status/{slug}` and `GET /monitors/{id}/uptime` in Redis with a short TTL (30–60s); invalidate (or just let TTL expire) on relevant writes.
- [ ] Rate limiting middleware (Redis-backed token bucket or fixed window) on write endpoints and especially on the public status endpoint to prevent abuse.
- [ ] Model: `ApiKey` (org_id, name, key_hash, last_used_at, created_at). `POST /orgs/{id}/api-keys`, `GET /orgs/{id}/api-keys`, `DELETE /api-keys/{id}`. A `require_api_key_or_jwt` dependency variant for endpoints you want scriptable (e.g. creating monitors from CI).
- [ ] Frontend: API keys management page (show key once on creation, never again — store only the hash server-side).

### DB changes
```sql
rollups_hourly(monitor_id FK, hour TIMESTAMPTZ, checks INT, failures INT, p95_ms INT,
                PRIMARY KEY(monitor_id, hour))
api_keys(id, org_id FK, name, key_hash, last_used_at NULL, created_at, revoked_at NULL)
```

### API endpoints
```
GET    /monitors/{id}/uptime?window=24h|7d|30d     # now reads from rollups where possible
POST   /orgs/{id}/api-keys
GET    /orgs/{id}/api-keys
DELETE /api-keys/{id}
```

### LLM prompting tips
- Ask for the rollup query and the retention job as separate, explicit SQL/queries you review yourself — this is a good place to *read* the generated SQL rather than trust it blindly, since a wrong `GROUP BY` silently produces plausible-looking but incorrect aggregates.
- For rate limiting, specify the exact algorithm you want (e.g. "Redis `INCR` + `EXPIRE` fixed window, 60 requests/minute per IP for the public endpoint") — "add rate limiting" alone tends to produce something under-specified.
- For API keys, insist the raw key is shown to the user exactly once at creation and only a hash is ever persisted — a common LLM shortcut is to store the plaintext key "for convenience."

### Acceptance test
Generate enough check history (or backfill test data) that `GET /monitors/{id}/uptime?window=30d` visibly reads from rollups (check query plan or add a temporary log line) rather than scanning all raw rows. Hammer `/public/status/{slug}` past the configured limit and confirm a 429. Create an API key, use it to create a monitor via `curl` with no browser session, then revoke it and confirm it stops working.

---

## Phase 7 — Frontend Polish & UX

**Goal:** The app feels like a real product, not a set of CRUD forms — worth screenshotting for your resume/portfolio.

### Tasks
- [ ] Consistent design system pass: spacing scale, color tokens (status green/red/gray), one card/table style reused everywhere.
- [ ] Dashboard/home: org-wide summary (monitors up/down count, recent incidents, quick-add monitor).
- [ ] Monitor detail: latency chart with time-range selector (24h/7d/30d), uptime bar visualization, incident history inline.
- [ ] Loading states (skeletons, not spinners-everywhere), empty states with clear calls to action, error boundaries with a friendly fallback.
- [ ] Toast notifications for actions (monitor created, channel test sent, etc.).
- [ ] Responsive layout check on mobile widths.
- [ ] Dark mode (optional but cheap with Tailwind + CSS variables, and a nice demo touch).
- [ ] Generate a typed API client from the FastAPI OpenAPI schema (e.g. `openapi-typescript`) instead of hand-writing `fetch` calls, so frontend/backend contracts can't silently drift.

### LLM prompting tips
- For visual design, give the LLM a short design brief (a couple of reference products, a color palette, "information-dense but not cluttered") rather than "make it look nice" — vague prompts produce generic Tailwind-default UI.
- Ask for the OpenAPI-generated types to be wired into TanStack Query hooks per resource (`useMonitors()`, `useMonitor(id)`, `useCreateMonitor()`) rather than calling `fetch` ad hoc in components — this is the pattern worth having muscle memory for.

### Acceptance test
A friend who has never seen the project can, without your help, find a monitor's uptime history and read an open incident's timeline within a minute of landing on the dashboard.

---

## Phase 8 — Testing, CI/CD, Deployment

**Goal:** Green CI on every push, and a live public URL you can put on your resume.

### Tasks
- [ ] Backend tests: unit tests for the SSRF guard, the checker's assertion logic, the RBAC dependency, and the scheduler's `next_run_at` math; integration tests (using a test DB, e.g. via `pytest` + a dockerized Postgres, or `testcontainers`) for the auth flow and monitor CRUD end to end.
- [ ] Frontend: a handful of component tests (Vitest + React Testing Library) for the auth form and the monitor form; optionally one end-to-end test (Playwright) covering "register → create monitor → see it in the list."
- [ ] `ruff` + `mypy` on the backend; `eslint` + `tsc --noEmit` on the frontend, all wired as CI steps that fail the build.
- [ ] GitHub Actions workflow: on push/PR — install deps, run linters, run backend tests against a service-container Postgres/Redis, run frontend tests and build, (on main branch only) build and push Docker images, deploy.
- [ ] Production `docker-compose.prod.yml` or platform-specific config (Render/Railway/Fly.io) for `api` + `worker` + managed Postgres + managed Redis.
- [ ] Frontend deployed separately (Vercel/Cloudflare Pages) pointing `VITE_API_URL` at the deployed API.
- [ ] Health checks configured on the hosting platform so it restarts a crashed `api` or `worker` container.
- [ ] Secrets (JWT secret, OAuth secrets, SMTP creds) set via the platform's secret manager, never committed.
- [ ] Final README pass: architecture diagram, live demo link, screenshots, "what I'd do differently at scale" section (this section specifically reads very well to reviewers).

### LLM prompting tips
- Write the CI workflow yourself first as a rough outline (which jobs, which order) and then ask the LLM to fill in the YAML — CI pipelines are one of the areas where an LLM will happily generate something plausible-but-wrong (bad caching keys, wrong working directories in a monorepo) if you don't constrain the shape first.
- Ask explicitly for **service containers** in the GitHub Actions job for Postgres/Redis rather than mocking the DB in integration tests — closer to how the app actually runs.

### Acceptance test
A fresh clone, with only `.env` filled in, passes `docker compose up --build` locally and a fresh push to `main` results in a green Actions run and an updated live deployment, without you touching the server manually.

---

## Phase 9 — Stretch / Advanced Features

Pick from these once the core is solid — don't start them early, they're easy rabbit holes.

- [ ] **SSL certificate expiry checks** — a monitor type that checks cert validity/expiry days remaining instead of an HTTP assertion.
- [ ] **Heartbeat ("dead man's switch") monitors** — an endpoint your own cron/job pings; PingBoard alerts if it *doesn't* hear from it within the expected window.
- [ ] **Multi-region checkers** — run the worker in more than one region/container and record which region a check came from, to distinguish "your server is down" from "one network path is down."
- [ ] **AI incident postmortem drafts** — an LLM call that takes an incident's timeline (start/end, affected monitor, check error messages) and drafts a plain-English postmortem summary for a human to edit before publishing; keep this explicitly a draft-and-edit feature, not an auto-published one.
- [ ] **Prometheus metrics endpoint** — expose `/metrics` for scraping, as a nod to real observability practice.
- [ ] **Maintenance windows** — suppress incident creation/alerts for a monitor during a scheduled window.

---

## Appendix C — Security Checklist

Go through this before calling any phase "done," and definitely before deploying publicly.

- [ ] Passwords hashed with bcrypt/argon2, never logged.
- [ ] JWT secret is long, random, and only in env vars/secret manager — never in code or committed `.env`.
- [ ] Refresh tokens stored hashed, rotated on use, revocable.
- [ ] SSRF guard on every user-supplied URL (monitors), re-checked on update, not just create.
- [ ] All org-scoped queries filter by `org_id` derived from the authenticated user's membership — never trust an `org_id` in the request body/query for authorization decisions, only for lookups.
- [ ] Public endpoints (`/public/status/{slug}`) use their own narrow response schema, reviewed for accidental data leakage.
- [ ] Rate limiting on auth endpoints (login/register) to slow brute force, separate from the general API rate limit.
- [ ] CORS configured to the actual frontend origin(s) only, not `*`, in production.
- [ ] Webhook/notification configs (Slack/Discord URLs) treated as secrets — not returned in full by `GET /channels` after creation (mask them).
- [ ] Dependency versions pinned; `pip-audit`/`npm audit` run at least once before deployment.
- [ ] HTTPS enforced end to end in production (frontend, API, and any webhook targets).

FILE: README.md
(321 lines)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# PingBoard

PingBoard is a multi-tenant uptime monitoring platform. An organization registers
monitors (a URL, a check interval and a set of assertions), a background worker
checks them on schedule, failures open incidents and notify the team over
email/Slack/Discord, and each org can publish a no-login public status page.

**Stack:** React + TypeScript + Tailwind · FastAPI (async) · PostgreSQL 16 · Redis 7 · ARQ · Docker · JWT/OAuth

## Architecture

```
                ┌──────────────┐
  browser ─────▶│  web (Vite)  │
                └──────┬───────┘
                       │  HTTPS / JSON
                ┌──────▼───────┐        ┌────────────┐
                │  api (FastAPI)├──────▶│  Postgres  │
                └──────┬───────┘        └─────▲──────┘
                       │  enqueue              │
                ┌──────▼───────┐               │
                │    Redis     │               │
                └──────┬───────┘               │
                       │  dequeue              │
                ┌──────▼───────┐               │
                │ worker (ARQ) ├───────────────┘
                └──────┬───────┘
                       │  HTTP checks / webhooks
                       ▼
             monitored targets · Slack · Discord · SMTP
```

The `api` and `worker` run the same image with different commands: the API never
performs outbound checks, and the worker never serves HTTP.

## Local setup

Requires Docker with the Compose plugin.

```bash
cp .env.example .env
# edit .env — at minimum set a real JWT_SECRET:
#   openssl rand -hex 32
docker compose up --build
```

| Service    | URL                            |
| ---------- | ------------------------------ |
| Frontend   | http://localhost:5173          |
| API        | http://localhost:8000          |
| API docs   | http://localhost:8000/docs     |
| Liveness   | http://localhost:8000/health   |
| Readiness  | http://localhost:8000/health/ready |

The frontend's landing page calls `/health` and renders the result, which
confirms the whole chain (browser → Vite → API → CORS) is wired up.

Postgres and Redis are published on **5433** and **6380** rather than their
standard ports, so the stack doesn't collide with a Postgres or Redis already
installed on the host. Override `POSTGRES_PORT` / `REDIS_PORT` in `.env` if
those are taken too; containers always reach each other on `db:5432` and
`redis:6379` regardless.

### Troubleshooting

**`PermissionError` reading files in `/app`** — on SELinux hosts (Fedora, RHEL)
bind mounts need the `:z` label, which `docker-compose.yml` already sets. If you
add a mount, label it too.

**Files created by containers are owned by root** — set `DOCKER_UID` /
`DOCKER_GID` in `.env` to your own (`id -u`, `id -g`) and rebuild; both images
take them as build args.

**`port is already allocated`** — something else holds the host port. Change
`POSTGRES_PORT`, `REDIS_PORT`, `API_PORT` or `WEB_PORT` in `.env`.

## Authentication

Sessions use a split-token scheme:

| | Access token | Refresh token |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Form | Signed JWT (HS256) | Opaque 64-byte random string |
| Lifetime | 15 minutes | 30 days |
| Where it lives | Client memory only | httpOnly `SameSite=Lax` cookie scoped to `/auth` |
| Server-side | Nothing stored | SHA-256 digest row, revocable |

The access token is deliberately kept out of `localStorage`: anything JavaScript
can read, injected script can read too. Because it lives only in memory, a page
reload starts with no token — the SPA silently calls `POST /auth/refresh`, which
the browser answers from the cookie it cannot itself read.

Refresh tokens are **single-use**. Every refresh revokes the presented token and
issues a new one, so a stolen cookie is only good until the legitimate client
refreshes next. Presenting an already-rotated token is treated as theft: every
outstanding session for that user is revoked and a warning is logged.

Passwords are bcrypt-hashed after a SHA-256 pre-hash, which sidesteps bcrypt's
72-byte truncation so long passphrases keep their full entropy.

`GET /auth/github/login` starts the GitHub authorization-code flow; the callback
sets the refresh cookie and redirects to the SPA, so no token is ever placed in a
URL. Leave `GITHUB_CLIENT_ID` / `GITHUB_CLIENT_SECRET` unset and the endpoints
return 503 — the rest of the app is unaffected.

## Roles

Every resource hangs off an organization, and access is decided by the caller's
membership, never by an `org_id` supplied in the request.

| Role | Can |
| --- | --- |
| `viewer` | Read the organization and its members |
| `admin` | …plus invite members (up to their own role) |
| `owner` | …plus everything, including granting `owner` |

Non-members get **404**, not 403: telling an outsider that an organization
exists is itself a disclosure.

## Monitors and the worker

A monitor is a URL, an interval and a set of assertions. The `worker` container
runs an ARQ scheduler that every 10s claims the monitors whose `next_run_at` has
passed, enqueues one job each, and advances their schedule.

Two details make that safe to scale past one worker:

- The claim uses `SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, so each due monitor is handed
  to exactly one worker and contention never blocks.
- `next_run_at` advances **as part of the claim**, before the check runs. A
  worker that dies mid-check costs one missed data point rather than re-claiming
  the same monitor in a tight loop against someone else's server.

After an outage the scheduler catches up in a single hop rather than firing every
slot it missed — a monitor on a 30s interval that was unchecked for an hour runs
once, not 120 times.

### Flap protection

A monitor flips to `down` only after `DEFAULT_FAILURE_THRESHOLD` (3) consecutive
failures, so one blip does not raise an alarm. Recovery is immediate: the first
success restores `up` and resets the counter. Both transitions are edge-triggered,
which is what Phase 4 uses to open and resolve incidents exactly once.

### SSRF, twice

User-supplied URLs are validated on create, on update, **and again inside the
worker** just before the request. DNS can change between saving a monitor and
checking it; re-resolving at execution time is what closes the rebinding hole.

## Incidents and alerting

When a monitor crosses the failure threshold the worker opens an `Incident`;
when it recovers, the same edge closes it and appends an automatic "resolved"
entry. A partial unique index (`monitor_id WHERE resolved_at IS NULL`) means the
database — not application logic — guarantees at most one open incident per
monitor, so two workers racing cannot produce duplicates.

Notifications are **queued, never inline**. The checker enqueues a job and moves
on; a webhook that hangs for 30 seconds can't slow down monitoring. Delivery
fans out to every active channel, and one broken channel does not stop the
others. The single exception is `POST /channels/{id}/test`, which delivers
synchronously because someone configuring a webhook needs to know immediately
whether it works.

Webhook URLs are **bearer credentials** — anyone holding one can post into that
workspace. They are stored as given but masked on every read, so `GET /channels`
returns `https://hooks.slack.com/…` and never the secret path.

## Public status pages

`GET /public/status/{slug}` takes no auth dependency and returns a schema
written for that endpoint alone — never `MonitorRead`. A visitor sees a display
name, a traffic light and a 90-day bar; the target URL, every UUID, the
assertions and the check interval all stay private, and a new monitor field is
private by default rather than exposed by accident.

On the frontend the route sits **outside** `AuthLayout`, so an anonymous visitor
triggers no session bootstrap and no call to `/auth/refresh` at all.

A missing slug and an unpublished page return the same 404, so the endpoint
cannot be used to discover private slugs. Monitors added to a page are filtered
through the owning org, so a guessed id from another tenant is silently dropped
rather than published.

Uptime is aggregated in SQL (`generate_series` left-joined to a grouped count),
not by pulling raw rows into Python: a 90-day window on a 30-second monitor is
~250k rows to produce 90 numbers. Days with no checks render as explicit gaps
rather than disappearing, so a monitoring outage never masquerades as uptime.

## Scale and safety

**Rollups.** A worker job aggregates each closed hour of `check_results` into
one `rollups_hourly` row per monitor, upserted on `(monitor_id, hour)` so a
re-run after a crash recomputes rather than double-counting. `GET
/monitors/{id}/uptime` reads those rollups plus the still-open hour, which on a
30-day window over 43k raw rows cut buffer reads from 841 to 15.

Counts recombine exactly; **percentiles do not**. The reported p95 is the worst
hour's p95 — a deliberate over-estimate rather than a figure that looks exact
and is quietly wrong. For a true p95, query raw rows inside the retention
window.

**Retention.** A daily job prunes raw checks older than `RAW_RETENTION_DAYS`
(30), in batches, and only rows already covered by a rollup — so pruning can
never destroy history that was not summarised first. Beyond that window,
history remains at hourly resolution. Raise the setting if per-check forensics
matter more than table size.

**Caching.** Public status pages (30s) and uptime figures (60s) are cached in
Redis, TTL-only with no invalidation: these are continuously-changing aggregates
where a short staleness window is invisible. A Redis outage degrades to
"slower", never "broken" — every path falls back to computing the value.
Responses carry `X-Cache: HIT|MISS`.

**Rate limits.** Redis fixed-window counters, bucketed by client IP, with three
separate budgets: auth endpoints (10/min, tightest — these are the ones worth
brute-forcing), the public status endpoint (60/min), and authenticated writes
(120/min). A Redis failure fails *open* on purpose: losing a rate limiter must
not lock users out of signing in.

**API keys.** `pb_`-prefixed (greppable, scanner-detectable), shown exactly
once, stored only as a SHA-256 digest. A key authenticates as an admin of one
organization and is refused against any other, so `POST /monitors` is scriptable
from CI without a browser session. Revoking is a soft delete, keeping the audit
trail after the credential dies.

## Frontend

**One token system.** Every colour is a role — `surface`, `ink`, `line`, and the
reserved status colours `up`/`down`/`degraded`/`unknown` — defined once in
`index.css`. Dark mode is a second set of values for those same roles under
`[data-theme="dark"]`, not a second set of components, and the latency chart
follows it through `var()` in SVG fills. Status is always word + shape, never
colour alone, so it survives colour-blindness and forced-colours mode.

**Typed against the backend.** `src/api/schema.d.ts` is generated from the live
OpenAPI document (`npm run generate:api`) and `types.ts` aliases those schemas
rather than restating them — so a backend field that changes shape fails `tsc`
instead of breaking at runtime. Wiring this up immediately caught two fields the
hand-written types had wrongly assumed were always present.

Loading states are skeletons shaped like the content they replace, empty states
name the next action, an error boundary catches render crashes, and actions
raise toasts. The layout is checked at 390px with no horizontal overflow, and
`prefers-reduced-motion` is honoured.

## Migrations

`docker compose up` runs `alembic upgrade head` before starting the API. To
drive Alembic by hand:

```bash
docker compose exec api alembic upgrade head
docker compose exec api alembic revision --autogenerate -m "add monitors"
docker compose exec api alembic downgrade -1
```
